### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Consumers Feel One Way, but Spend Another
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
Americans have spent much of the past several years telling surveys they are cautious
about the economy. Yet they continue to spend as though they are not. The University
of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index remains near multidecade lows, while the
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence has also been in a yearslong downtrend.

These surveys reflect a list of persistent concerns from elevated food and gas prices to
geopolitical uncertainty and labor market anxiety.
Even so, consumer spending has remained remarkably resilient. The final estimate of Q2
real consumer spending, as measured by PCE, was revised up to an annualized rate of
3.8%, well above the 2.3% 20-year average. More recent data suggest that momentum
has continued. According to Bank of America Institute, the latest monthly total credit and

debit card spending data per household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY) in September, and
spending excluding gasoline was up 5.1% YoY, both the third strongest growth rates in the
past four years. 1
The persistence of consumer spending is particularly notable given that temporary cyclical
boosts like larger tax refunds are starting to fade. That raises an important question: What is
actually funding strong consumption today? Recent data suggest the drivers are becoming

increasingly more diversified, with broadening labor market trends, healthy consumer
finances and elevated wealth effects all reinforcing one another and underpinning consumers.
First, the labor market continues to provide an important foundation for consumer spending,
and recent trends suggest wage growth is supporting a broader set of households than in
recent years. Jobs growth has slowed from the exceptionally strong pace seen following the
pandemic, but broader indicators suggest labor market conditions remain relatively stable.

Although the September jobs report came in softer than expected, the three- and six-month
rolling averages indicate that the trend in jobs growth likely bottomed out last year and has
gradually improved since (Exhibit 1A). Meanwhile, the unemployment rate remains low by
historical standards and layoffs remain contained. And while wage growth has moderated
and recently trailed headline inflation (Exhibit 1B), cumulative income gains over the past
several years have likely helped many households absorb higher prices better.

An important development though has been improving wage growth trends among lower-
income households, where spending tends to be most sensitive to changes in wages. Bank
of America Institute consumer deposit account data show after-tax wage growth for lower-
income households outpaced higher-income households for a second consecutive month
in August, rising 4.7% YoY compared with 3.5% for higher-income households. Importantly,
this improvement appears to be supporting discretionary spending, not just spending

on necessities. Lower-income discretionary spending rose 5.7% YoY in August, nearly
matching the 5.9% pace for higher-income households. In other words, the narrowing gap
in wage and spending growth across income cohorts suggests consumer resilience looks
to be becoming less dependent on affluent households alone, creating a broader and more
durable foundation for consumption.
Second, household finances remain in relatively good shape, providing consumers with the

flexibility to continue spending. The ratio of household debt to disposable personal income
remains near its lowest level since the late 1990s, excluding the temporary distortions
caused by pandemic-related income support in 2020 and 2021 (Exhibit 1C). Recent
revisions to the Bureau of Economic Analysis national income accounts also suggest
consumers have a larger financial buffer than previous data suggested. Personal saving
estimates over the past year have been revised higher by $320 billion, or 39%, and the

revised savings rate remains above 4% after previous estimates showed it fell to 3% during
the summer.2 Concerns that consumers may be rapidly exhausting their savings buffers
may therefore be overstated. Supporting this view, Bank of America Institute consumer
1 Bank of America Institute, “Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers.” As of October 9, 2026.
2 Bank of America, “Massive Savings Markup = Another Reason to Be Bullish.” As of October 2, 2026.

Investment Implications
Consumer spending strength
remains a key pillar of economic
and earnings growth, which keeps
us constructive on our Equity
overweight. The growing connection
between household wealth and
spending reinforces the importance
of maintaining a diversified portfolio.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
deposit account data show that middle- and lower-income households continue to maintain
deposit balances above prepandemic levels across age groups.
Third, elevated household wealth continues to provide a powerful tailwind for consumer
spending, particularly among households that account for an outsized share of overall
consumption. U.S. household net worth rose $12.8 trillion (T) to a record $195.9T in Q2,

driven largely by gains in Equities, which contributed $10.7T of the increase (Exhibit 1D).
As a result, household net worth relative to disposable income climbed to a record 8.3,
meaning households now hold more than eight dollars of wealth for every dollar of annual
disposable income. Households are also sitting on $35.8T in home equity, an all-time high
level that provides a significant balance sheet cushion. Rising asset values have helped to

support spending through what economists refer to as wealth effects. In other words, when
households feel wealthier, they tend to be more willing to spend.
For investors, the key takeaway is that consumer spending is being supported by multiple
engines despite the weaker sentiment data. Labor income trends are supporting wider
participation, healthy balance sheets are helping households absorb economic pressures,
and elevated household wealth continues to sustain higher-income spending. Together,

these forces are supportive of economic growth and corporate profits, which is particularly
important since the consumer accounts for roughly 70% of U.S. gross domestic product.
That said, it does not eliminate risk. A meaningful risk to the consumer would be a scenario
in which both the labor and wealth engines weaken simultaneously. For now, labor market
conditions remain stable, and wealth effects remain supportive. Equity market pullbacks in
recent years have done little to derail consumer spending, suggesting it would likely take a

deeper and more prolonged drawdown to alter consumer behavior.

MARKET VIEW
The Bull Market Turns Four: Older, Unloved—But Still Kicking
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Four years ago this month, virtually no one was in the mood to buy U.S. Equities, and for
good reasons.
Rewind to October 2022: Inflation the month before clocked in at an annual rate of 8.2%.

The yield curve was inverted, a signal that has preceded most past recessions. Consumer
sentiment was abysmal, having dropped to a record low in June 2022. Recession
concerns were mounting, with an October Wall Street Journal poll survey putting the
odds of a recession within 12 months at about 63%. And realizing (belatedly) that
inflation was not “transitory,” the Fed was in the midst of administering one of the most
aggressive monetary tightening cycles in decades, raising the fed funds rate five times

between March and September 2022 by a stunning 300 basis points.
Reflecting the mood at the moment, the S&P 500 closed at 3,577 on October 12, 2022,
down 25% below its January peak, leaving investors battered and bruised but blind to the
fact that the market had bottomed. Four years on, the bull market that emerged from
the gloom of 2022 is still standing and kicking, with the S&P 500 up roughly 130% since
the October 2022 lows.
The more than doubling of the S&P over the past four years has hardly been linear—and

has been accompanied with plenty of pain and doubt. Exhibit 2 maps the rocky path
trodden by the bull over the past four years. Less than six months into the rally, the
markets were rocked by the failure of Silicon Valley Bank (SVB) on March 10, 2023. The
S&P Regional Banks Select Industry Index lost 18% that week and stoked concerns of
massive deposit runs and credit stress across the capital markets. However, owing to
emergency intervention by the government, lower yields and strength elsewhere in the

markets, the crisis passed. Some banks failed that month, but the bull market didn’t.
Exhibit 2: Revisiting the Rocky Path Trodden by the Bull.
Period "Reason to Sell" What happened instead
October 2022 Inflation >8%, aggressive Fed tightening, recession concerns Bull market begins
March 2023 SVB/regional banking crisis Stocks keep climbing
2023-24 Inverted yield curve, “recession inevitable” Economy keeps expanding
2024 “Magnificent 7* bubble,” narrow breadth, expensive valuations Earnings propel market higher

2025 Tariffs, trade war, policy uncertainty Market absorbs another shock
2026 Oil/diesel shock, 5%+ yields, Fed tightening, geopolitical risks Bull reaches fourth birthday
*Apple, Amazon, Alphabet, Nvidia, Meta, Microsoft and Tesla. Source: Chief Investment Office. As of October 9, 2026.
But recessionary concerns hung like threatening clouds over the markets for most of
2023. Aggressive Fed tightening, an inverted yield curve, and soft economic data were

all taken as harbingers of an economic slowdown and profits recession. Yet the S&P 500
continued to climb in 2023, up 26.3% for the year, driven in large part by the outsized
price performance of the Magnificent Seven.
Investors spent much of 2024 fretting about a market too dependent on technology,
and therefore a market too narrow and too expensive. The unwinding of the Japan carry
trade in August 2024 engendered more angst, as did uncertainties around the 2024

U.S. presidential election. But despite a host of misgivings, the S&P 500 delivered total
returns of 25% in 2024. The unloved bull marched on.
Total returns in 2025 were robust as well—roughly 18%—notwithstanding—yet
again—a number of headwinds. In late January, DeepSeek, a cheaper China AI model,
raised questions about America’s technology leadership and the need to spend enormous
sums on chips and data centers. The Nasdaq promptly dropped by 3.1% thereafter.

Investment Implications
The adoption phase of the AI buildout
should translate to broader earnings
growth and performance across
sectors, reinforcing our conviction
in U.S. Equities. Given a strong
fundamental backdrop, we would
continue to buy on weakness as the
bull enters its fifth year

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
January’s jolt was followed by “Liberation Day,” or the warning of punishing U.S. tariffs
on America’s major trading partners. The trade-policy shock of April 2025 caused the
S&P 500 to drop nearly 19% from its February high to its April 8 low on uncertainty
that higher import costs would decrease corporate margins and consumer spending and
upend global supply chains. Amid the trade tumult, Treasury prices fell and the U.S. dollar

weakened on concerns that foreigners were abandoning U.S. assets. Remember all the
angst over the dollar debasement trade? If that wasn’t enough, expensive valuations and
enormous capital commitments raised doubts about the AI trade and triggered talk of an
AI bubble. Yet despite this wall of worry, the S&P 500, for the third year running, posted
double-digit gains in 2025.
And double-digit gains could be in store this year, with the S&P 500 up 14% through

October 9. The bull has rumbled on, taking in stride the energy shock from the war in
the Middle East, the shift in interest rate expectations from the Fed (from rate cuts
to rate hikes), and the rising uncertainty associated with the midterm elections. These
headwinds have been countered by tailwinds such as better-than-expected corporate
earnings and better-than-anticipated real growth.
Finally, as Exhibit 3 makes clear, what has given this bull so much muscle has been

the U.S. Technology sector, specifically the Magnificent 7. Powering the bull has been
the Magnificent 7, with a staggering 344% return since the 2022 lows, while U.S.
technology has delivered a 277% return versus the S&P 500’s return of 130%. Note the
outperformance of the S&P 500 relative to the main international indices.
Looking forward: Is the bull’s greatest strength also its Achilles’ heel? Bull
markets don’t die of natural causes. And they typically don’t expire because of one

particular signal or indicator. Bull markets turn to bear markets when a cluster of signals/
metrics turn south. Think deteriorating and downward earnings revisions, higher interest
rates, widening credit spreads and tighter lending standards, growth disappointments,
stretched valuations and sentiment, deteriorating market breadth, and an external
shock. Any one of these signals could flash red and not trouble the bull.  But when they
converge or feed off each other, that’s trouble.

In addition, since a great deal of the earnings growth, market leadership and capital
expenditures (capex) spending of this bull market have come from the technology sector,
the bull’s greatest strength could become its greatest vulnerability. AI has become an
investment ecosystem unto itself, and hence the fifth year of the bull market will pivot
in part around AI revenue, frontier model adoption rates, infrastructure spending, free
cash flows, depreciation costs, debt issuance, data center utilization, cloud growth, and

competition via cheaper models and computing alternatives. To put it mildly, there is a
great deal at stake when it comes to the bull and AI.
However, this is where we stand today: We believe the bull will continue to rumble
ahead next year, with AI revenues and productivity gains justifying the costs of the AI
infrastructure. Critically, AI is moving from the buildout phase, dominated by chipmakers
and hyperscaler capex, into the adoption phase, where software firms, healthcare,

finance, energy and a myriad of other firms start to see productivity gains and margin
expansion from rising AI adoption rates. We believe this will give better breadth to the
S&P 500; so too will continued profit growth across various U.S. sectors, AI investment
that starts to pay off, and an economy capable of absorbing setbacks or periodic bouts
of uncertainty.
The path forward won’t be linear—and there will be fits and starts, and market
drawdowns. That’s normal for any market. But that aside, the bull will keep rumbling,

roaming and kicking in 2027.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is our view of the macro and market landscape heading
into 2027 and beyond?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
A supply side-led economic expansion continues, driven by capital investment in the AI
buildout, solid consumer spending and fiscal tailwinds still blowing, albeit with less force.
This creates a backdrop that keeps nominal growth well-above trend and, importantly,

above the interest cost on national debt.
The interest rate backdrop largely remains the same as of today, but, instead of the
sharp increases that we experienced in 2026, the curve should settle down, and yields
should remain relatively range bound. The Fed is likely to tap on the breaks a couple of
more times before reassessing new inflation, growth and employment trends later in
2027.
Concerns surrounding credit risk and "circular" financing are likely to rise the further we

move into the AI buildout, but we do not expect a default cycle to develop. Rather, we
expect the sharp backup in yields to place pressure namely on lower-quality credits in
areas having a difficult time building the cash flow necessary to pay the higher interest
costs.
We expect a broadening out within the market but technology to still maintain its
heavyweight positioning. As we move from training and evolving the large language
models to agentic, and physical AI new leaders are likely to develop. In addition, we

anticipate AI adopters such as biotechnology, transport, logistics, advance manufacturing
and financial companies to enter new growth and productivity phases.
Lastly, overall earnings growth in the U.S. should be low to mid double-digit percent
stages in 2027, which is about half of the growth from 2026 thus far but still around
double from normal levels.
Credit risks developing and higher energy prices for longer are the main risks to our bull
case for Equities extending further into 2027 and beyond.

Index Definitions
ecurities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

University of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index is a monthly survey of consumer confidence levels in the United States conducted by the University of Michigan.
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence measures what consumers are feeling about their expected financial situation, whether that's optimistic or pessimistic.
Consumer Price Index is a statistical measure that tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative "market basket" of everyday goods and

services.
Nasdaq Composite Index is a stock market measure that tracks the performance of more than 3,000 stocks listed on the Nasdaq exchange.
MSCI Emerging Markets c is a stock market index that tracks the performance of large-cap and mid-cap companies across 24 developing and fast-growing nations.
MSCI All-Country World Index ex USA is a market-capitalization-weighted stock market index that tracks large- and mid-cap equity performance across developed and emerging markets

worldwide, excluding the United States.
MSCI Europe, Australasia, and the Far East (EAFE) Index is a stock market index that measures the performance of large- and mid-cap companies across 21 developed.
Dow Jones Industrial Average Index is a stock market index that tracks 30 major public companies listed on the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) and Nasdaq.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index is an alternative version of the standard S&P 500 that gives every constituent company an identical weight of 0.2% during each quarterly rebalance, rather

than weighting companies by their market capitalization.
Russell 2000 Index is a stock market index that tracks the performance of approximately 2,000 small-cap (small-capitalization) public companies in the United States.
S&P 500 sub-sectors and industry groups Global Industry Classification Standard (GICS®)/S&P 500 Total Return Index, including Information Technology Total Return (TR) USD;
Consumer Discretionary TR USD; Industrials TR USD; Real Estate TR USD; Communication Services TR USD; Materials  TR USD; Financials TR USD; Consumer Staples TR USD; Utilities TR USD;

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

Economy 
Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers 
09 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumer spending remained very solid in September. Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third strongest growth rates over the past four 
years. Month-over-month (MoM) spending was more muted, especially without gas. 
• Spending shows considerable breadth: the vast majority of discretionary retail and services categories are showing growth. 
Moreover, this strength extends across income cohorts, with the ongoing recovery of lower- and middle-income households' 
spending growth. 
• With the winter months fast approaching, holiday spending is already increasing at a solid rate, though it's still early. In Bank of 
America deposit data through September, buy now, pay later (BNPL) usage is rising but not at a rate that is overly concerning. 
However, nearly one in every five households that use BNPL has a repayment of over $500.

Consumer Checkpoint is a regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It aims to provide a holistic and real-time estimate of US 
consumers’ spending and their financial well-being, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America proprietary data. Such data is not 
intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, financial conditions or performance of 
Bank of America. 
A solid September for spending   
Consumer spending continued to look solid in September. Bank of America total aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), while spending excluding gasoline increased 5.1% YoY. Notably, both are the third 
strongest growth rates in the past four years. Seasonally-adjusted total card spending rose 0.2% month-over-month (MoM), 
while spending excluding gasoline was little changed. Overall, spending growth remains well above the 2025 average rate 
(Exhibit 1). 
Exhibit 1: Total card spending per household rose 6.2% YoY and 5.1% YoY excluding gasoline in September 
Total card spending growth per household, based on Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card data (monthly, MoM%, seasonally adjusted (SA)) and

Most spending categories are growing  
One striking thing about recent card spending, in our view, is that it is well balanced across categories. For example, both 
spending on retail (excluding gasoline) and services grew strongly YoY in September, at 3.5% and 5.4%, respectively. Meanwhile, 
retail spending is showing notably better momentum than in 2025 (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: Spending is rising across retail and services 
Retail (excluding gasoline) and services card spending per household (monthly, seasonally adjusted, index January 2025 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Well balanced across “nice-to-have” and “need-to-have” retail categories  
Is the momentum in retail spending reflective of “nice-to-have” discretionary or “need-to-have” necessity spending? In retail, 
this is a nuanced question, as many categories sit in a gray area between “necessity” and “discretionary”. General merchandise 
stores, for example, sell both essentials like groceries as well as nice-to-have electronics or even jewelry.  
In Exhibit 3, we account for this by grouping retail spending into “more discretionary” (furniture, electronics, building materials, 
online retailers, clothing retailers and miscellaneous stores), “partly discretionary” (auto, health and personal care retailers, 
general merchandise) and “mostly necessity” (gas and groceries). Even within these categories the edges will be blurred, but the 
overall impression is that retail spending growth is widely distributed, with spending rising across all fronts. One caveat: “mostly 
discretionary” spending has been making a smaller contribution since June. 
Exhibit 3: Retail spending is distributed across discretionary and necessity categories  
Percentage points contribution to YoY growth by type of retail spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data. See methodology for category aggregation definitions.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking at the share of “more discretionary” retail spending categories that are increasing YoY underscores that growth is well 
balanced and broad-based (Exhibit 4). Non-store and online retailers and miscellaneous stores saw some of the fastest growth, 
while furniture and building materials were among the laggards, though other durable categories showed some strength.

While some of this spending growth likely reflects the impact of higher prices pushing up spending across a swathe of 
categories, looking just at transactions as a proxy for volumes shows a similar picture, with only slightly less diffusion (Exhibit 5).

For services, growth is driven mostly by discretionary spending  
In services spending, on the other hand, the boundaries between discretionary and necessity spending are often clearer. Travel 
spending (e.g., airlines, lodging) can be thought of as, by and large, discretionary. At the same time, utility bills, rent and 
insurance payments are necessities.  
And in services, discretionary spending remains the clear driver, accounting for over three-quarters of YoY spending growth in 
September (Exhibit 6). Leisure and housing services are major components of growth, though travel and restaurants also made 
positive contributions. However, as we noted last month strength in airline spending largely reflects price rises rather than 
transactions growth (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling).  
Exhibit 6: Services spending growth has been mostly driven by discretionary spending over the past year 
Percentage points contribution to growth by type of services spending (three-month moving average)

Lower-income households’ spending outpaced all but the top 5% 
The breadth in spending growth across categories is also evident across income cohorts. In September, lower-income 
households’ total card spending growth was 5.5% YoY, while the equivalent figures for middle- and higher-income households 
were 4.9% and 4.8% YoY, respectively. The top 5% of households by income continue to show the fastest spending growth, 
5.9% in September, though this has eased somewhat (Exhibit 7). 
Overall, the divergences across income cohorts are much smaller than they were in 2025 and the first three months of this year. 
Just focusing on spending excluding key necessities (gasoline, food and utilities) doesn’t meaningfully change the picture of 
convergence across income cohorts (Exhibit 8).  
Exhibit 7: The top 5% saw the strongest spending growth of any 
income cohort… 
Total card spending per household by income tercile and the top 5% 
(three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 8: …though discretionary spending for the top 5% has eased 
some since June 
Discretionary card spending per household by income tercile and the top 
5% (three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The strength in lower- and middle-income households’ discretionary spending is occurring despite necessity spending shifting 
from a tailwind to a headwind over the past year amid rising gas prices (Exhibit 9). In our view, a key component of why these 
households have been able to combine rising necessity spending with strength in discretionary is the labor market and a 
recovery in their after-tax wage growth (Exhibit 10).  
In September, lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY from 4.7% YoY in August, while growth for middle- and higher-
income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. These changes were modest, and lower-income households are 
continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” pattern we 
observed over much of 2025 and 2026.  
Exhibit 9: Necessity spending has gone from a tailwind to a 
headwind across all income cohorts

Should we be concerned about the easing in household spending and after-tax wage growth for those with the top 5% of 
incomes? For now, probably not, as in our view this spending growth is likely being driven by wealth effects rather than wage 
gains. Absent a sharp correction in equity markets, there is little reason to expect them to retrench their spending. However, 
developments here are worth watching as this cohort comprises a significant proportion of overall consumer spending. They also 
tend to make outsized contributions to several discretionary spending categories (Exhibit 11).  
Exhibit 11: The top 5% of households by income make up a disproportionate amount of spending in highly discretionary categories 
Share of spending by the top 5% of households by income across select discretionary categories (three-month moving average to September 2026, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
With summer gone, thoughts turn to the holidays and BNPL… 
Now that summer is over, the next big gauge of consumer momentum is how people spend during the run-up to, and over, the 
holiday season. It is early days, but September spending on holiday items ran well ahead of 2025 (Exhibit 12). Given the backdrop 
of firmer after-tax wage growth and breadth across retail and services categories, this is perhaps not surprising. However, some 
of this spending strength could be due to rising prices as opposed to more purchases.  
Exhibit 12: It’s early days, but holiday spending is strong so far this year

A concern often raised as we move into the holidays is whether consumers will lean on less traditional forms of credit such as 
buy now, pay later (BNPL) to finance their spending. Frequent BNPL users tend to have higher credit card utilization rates (read 
more in: Do consumers have wiggle room to absorb higher gas prices?).  
Looking at the share of households in Bank of America deposit data with a BNPL payment, we do tend to see a seasonal rise 
over the holidays (Exhibit 13), though in past years, these increases have quickly reversed. Further, while the underlying trend in 
BNPL usage is upwards, there are no signs of a sharp acceleration. We do, however, see some evidence that BNPL is being used 
to finance more expensive purchases, with the share of households making repayments above $500 rising, albeit relatively slowly 
(Exhibit 14).  
Exhibit 13: 8.3% of Bank of America households had a BNPL 
payment in September 
Share of households with a BNPL payment in the month (monthly, %)

repayment of over $500

(monthly, $ amounts)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The tendency towards greater usage is occurring across income cohorts, though is somewhat more pronounced for lower-
income households (Exhibit 15). However, higher-income households have the largest share of BNPL payments above $500 
(Exhibit 16). Overall, in our view, the increase in the share of BNPL users heading into the season does not appear too 
concerning, though the rate of usage for some BNPL consumers does bear watching.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 289: Expected 2027 TMT supply points to a new record

Source PDF: all report pdf/Expected 2027 TMT supply points to a new record.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

European TMT gross bond supply may reach €150bn in 2027,
driven by tech funding for AI and data centres rather than
traditional telecoms. Strong cash ﬂows and credit quality
support issuance, but disciplined funding will be key to market
absorption
Jan Frederik Slijkerman
AI and data centre investment is expected to be the main driver of TMT bond
issuance in 2027
We predict another year of strong supply for the European TMT
sector. We expect €150bn of gross issuance and €103bn of net

issuance, given €47bn of debt redemptions. The additional
issuance is not coming from the traditional telecom or media
issuers, but from global tech companies.
As we expect the data centre build-out to continue in 2027, issuers
are likely to return to the European investment-grade credit market
to raise part of the required funding. Meanwhile, we do not see a
major reason for traditional sectors to reduce or increase

Telecoms supply
We will break down the main factors aﬀecting debt issuance,
starting with FY27 capex requirements. While we expect capex as a
percentage of sales to decline, aggregate capex is likely to increase

slightly. Companies such as Orange and Vodafone are integrating
recent acquisitions and may need to invest additional capital to
realise merger synergies. We therefore do not expect the lower
capex-to-sales ratio to translate into less issuance. Nevertheless,
BT and KPN are exceptions, as both clearly intend to reduce capex
in FY27.
Low-single-digit EBITDA growth gives companies some capacity to
issue additional debt while maintaining stable leverage. However,

higher interest rates provide an incentive to keep debt levels in
check. We therefore expect the increase in debt issuance to be
driven mainly by M&A. Several debt-funded acquisitions of smaller
networks could generate around €5bn of net issuance. A further
€3bn could come from US operators raising euro-denominated
funding for spectrum payments in 2027.

### Page 3

Technology supply
In 2027, we believe the AI build-out will continue. Tech companies
currently report healthy returns on investment, demand for AI
services continues to outpace supply, and companies want to
maintain their market positions. We therefore expect further capex
growth in 2027, with the large hyperscalers investing close to
US$1tr in ﬁscal 2027.
As in 2026, we expect these companies to ﬁnance their
investments largely from operating cash ﬂow, but also through

debt issuance, with additional funding coming from European
markets. We expect hyperscaler data centre operators to issue at
least €60bn in 2027, somewhat above the approximately €35bn
issued year-to-date in 2026, including issuance by NTT.
In addition to the €60bn of issuance from hyperscaler data centre
operators, we expect €35bn of net supply from other technology
issuers. Companies are likely to pursue acquisitions and raise
additional funding for investment, including semiconductor,

software and payment services companies. Including €8bn from
telecom issuers, this brings total TMT net supply to €103bn. This
would translate into gross supply of approximately €150bn, which,
in our view, is close to the amount the European market may be
able to absorb.
Supply discipline will be important
Companies will need to issue prudently and provide clear guidance
on future funding plans, as spreads may only tighten once the
market no longer expects substantial new supply. Nevertheless,

current spreads look attractive relative to the issuers’ high ratings,
with much of the widening driven by supply and liquidity eﬀects.
Relatively wide CDS spreads also suggest that technical factors,
rather than a material deterioration in credit risk, are weighing on
valuations. This combination of high credit quality and attractive
spreads should attract new money to the investment-grade
market.

Challenge 1: Does debt issuance reduce
credit quality?
First, we expect the hyperscaler data centre operators Amazon,
Microsoft, Alphabet, Meta and Oracle to grow their combined
EBITDA by US$230bn, or 29%, in FY27, to around US$1tr. Moreover,
with the exception of Oracle, these companies are expected to
operate with limited net debt, with Microsoft holding more cash
than debt. The main caveat is their signiﬁcant lease commitments,
many of which are not yet reﬂected in balance-sheet lease

rating agencies will attach greater weight to these liabilities,
particularly if returns on investment start to decline. In our view, this
is not a material concern for 2027 and may never become one,
given the sector’s strong growth and ability to fund a signiﬁcant
part of investment from operating cash ﬂow. Companies also retain
ﬂexibility to scale back future investment that has not yet been
committed if returns start to disappoint.
Challenge 2: Do credit benchmarks

accommodate more supply?
Do European credit markets have the capacity to absorb additional
supply from technology companies? We think the current share of
technology issuers remains modest. Alphabet accounts for only
1.16% of the iBoxx € Non-Financials index, while Amazon and IBM
account for 0.68% and 0.91%, respectively.
In the US, Oracle’s share of more than 2% in the iBoxx $ Domestic
Non-Financials index illustrates that individual technology issuers

can reach materially higher benchmark weights, while several high-

## Document 324: Kicking and screaming_ Child care costs persist Child care remains a constraint for many families’ budgets, with the average share of monthly child care 8% of a household’s pay. 2 

Source PDF: all report pdf/Kicking and screaming_ Child care costs persist Child care remains a constraint for many families’ budgets, with the average share of monthly child care 8% of a household’s pay. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13030323     1

Economy 
Kicking and screaming: Child care costs persist 
20 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Child care remains a growing financial burden for many households, even as growth in the median payment has moderated, 
according to Bank of America payments data. More families are paying more than $2,000 per month for care, suggesting 
affordability pressures are becoming increasingly concentrated among households facing the highest expenses. 
• Child care cost pressures vary significantly across the country. Nashville and Jacksonville, FL posted the fastest year-over-year 
growth among major metro areas, while New York and Washington DC saw the biggest decrease in median payment growth, 
according to Bank of America payments data. 
• Limited supply may continue to constrain access to child care for some households. Child care employment fell 0.7% year-over-
year in September, suggesting provider capacity remains under pressure and could continue to affect household finances and 
labor force participation.

Child care costs outpace rent and medical services 
Child care remains a significant expense for many families. In September, the growth in child care and other school-related fees 
surpassed other major household expenses such as rent and medical services, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) 
(Exhibit 1). 
Yet, while child care prices continue to rise in official inflation data, median household payments have largely moderated since 
February, according to Bank of America data (Exhibit 2). More importantly, the growth in the number of households paying for 
child care has fallen since the start of 2025. 
Exhibit 1: The cost of child care has surpassed other services such as 
rent in the consumer price index

changing the amount of care they use, which is reflected in slower growth in the number paying for child care. As more

households shift into lower-cost care (school-age, public pre-K, subsidized or free after-school programs), median payments can

The decline in the number of households paying for child care may also reflect families seeking alternatives to formal care 
arrangements. Some may be relying more heavily on relatives, shifting parents’ work schedules or dropping out of the workforce 
altogether to care for their children in response to elevated costs. This could explain why, among all income groups paying for 
care, the number of lower-income households has seen the biggest decline, falling 2.0% year-over-year (YoY) in September 
(Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: The number of lower-income households paying for child care has fallen the most, down 2.0% YoY in September 
Number of households with child care payments (monthly, three-month moving average, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
More families face larger monthly bills 
Yet even as fewer households pay for child care, the share of those shelling out more than $2,000 per month has increased 
substantially (Exhibit 4). In fact, it has more than doubled since 2023, reaching 7.7% in 2026 year-to-date. Meanwhile, the share 
of those paying $1,000 or less has declined, suggesting higher monthly payments are becoming more common even as the 
national median grows more slowly. 
Exhibit 4: The share of households paying >$2000 a month for child care has more than doubled since 2023 
Monthly child care payments by amount (annual average, share of households, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Rising costs take a bigger bite out of households’ paychecks 
What makes child care “affordable”? According to the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, child care is considered 
affordable if it accounts for no more than 7% of family income. Yet, in Bank of America data, the average child care cost was 8% 
in September.  
While an 8% share may not appear dramatically above the federal affordability benchmark of 7%, it represents an average across 
households’ income levels. For families in high-cost metro areas or those paying more than $2,000 per month, the burden is 
likely considerably higher.

Where child care costs are climbing 
Child care pressures also differ substantially by location. In Bank of America payments data, Nashville posted the fastest YoY 
median payment growth in September, followed by Jacksonville, FL and Phoenix (Exhibit 6). This dispersion of growth across the 
country suggests child care cost pressures are no longer confined to historically expensive coastal markets.  
Interestingly, New York and Washington DC saw the sharpest decline in growth this year. Why might this be? The supply side of 
the market may help explain why – for example, New York City recently introduced universal Pre-K.1

Fewer providers, higher costs 
As of September 2026, employment in child care services was down 0.7% from a year earlier, suggesting the sector’s workforce 
recovery has stalled. Fewer workers can constrain the number of available places or the hours providers offer. That may leave 
families facing longer searches, fewer workable arrangements or continued price pressure. In turn, access to care can influence 
parents’ ability to enter, remain in or expand their participation in the workforce. 
Exhibit 7: Employment within child care services fell 0.7% YoY in September 
Employees by sector (monthly, seasonally adjusted, YoY%)

Source: Haver Analytics  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
What are the economic implications of rising child care costs? 
Child care costs may have important labor market implications for families with young children. Of those households paying for 
child care in 2026, more than half of them were Older Millennials (52%), followed by Younger Millennials (20%) and Gen X (16%), 
according to Bank of America payments data. This means child care costs weigh more heavily on Older Millennials. 
The share of Older Millennial households receiving more than one paycheck has continued to grow from a year earlier, but the 
pace of that improvement has moderated over the past three months (Exhibit 8). Given that this group accounts for the majority 
of households paying for child care in Bank of America payments data, they may offer early signs of how affordability affects 
their employment decisions. 
Exhibit 8: The share of Older Millennial households paying for child care that have more than one person with a paycheck has declined since 2021  
Share of households with a child care payment with more than one person with a paycheck (%, monthly, three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Child care challenges can weigh on women’s employment 
According to data from the American Time Use Survey, women continue to perform significantly more child care and household 
work than men, even as their participation in paid work has increased. This suggests that persistent gaps in access and 
affordability may have disproportionate consequences for women’s employment. Indeed, the percentage of women who cited 
child care as a reason for temporary unemployment was nearly three times higher than men’s in 2026, though the average fell 
from 2025, according to data from the BLS. 
Yet labor force participation data indicates a more nuanced picture. Despite persistent child care pressures, mothers are 
participating in the workforce at rates that remain above their 2019 averages.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Bank of America credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card holders 
making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards is excluded. Data regarding 
merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial services 
companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System (NAICS), 
which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
If applicable, the consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level.  
If applicable, any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a 
deposit account or credit card. Any reference to aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-
peer, cash and checks.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Economist, Bank of America Institute

萬台 GB300 ，2027 年目標則為 13 GW ，將以 Vera Rubin 為主，預期

及 Space 三大部門。 Connectivity 以 Starlink 衛星連網服務為核心，涵

Falcon 9 開啟火箭技術革命 ： 傳統 火箭多為一次性 設計 ，主要結構於
發 射後 拋棄， 使每 次發射 任務 均須重 新製 造與整 備， 導致 火 箭發 射成
本高昂。而 SpaceX 的獵鷹 9 號(Falcon 9 )於 2015 年首次完成一級火箭
回收，並於 2017 年實現回收助推器再次飛行，突破火箭一次性使用限
制，降低火箭發射成本， 使衛星寬頻通訊商業化可行性提高。 Falcon 9
每次發射衛星 報價 約在 3,200 美元 /公斤 ，SpaceX 自用成本更低 ，而最
新 的傳 統火 箭(Ariane 6) 發射 報價 則 約在 6,000 美元 /公斤 ， 相比 之 下
SpaceX 在火箭發射成本上有顯著優勢。 而 下 一 代 火 箭星艦 (Starship)
推測每公斤成本約在 600 美元 /公斤， 且目標 為全部回收並 將發射成本
進一步降低至 200 美元 /公斤 ，有望進一步帶動衛星發射數量 。

## Document 406: ‘Cost-Plus’ Costs (2026-10-25)

Source PDF: all report pdf/‘Cost-Plus’ Costs (2026-10-25).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-25
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #luciellis #labour #rba #productivity
‘Cost-Plus’ Costs
By Luci Ellis
Chief Economist Westpac Group, Westpac
10:40 October 16 2026 Share
Entrenched expectations that cost increases can and should be passed on are precisely
what tight monetary policy is leaning against, but only indirectly.
The recent tendency of some firms to bundle into prices card-acceptance costs that had previously
been surcharged – and then some, in some cases – demonstrates a ‘cost-plus’ approach to pricing.

### Page 2

The recent banning of card surcharges has caused considerable upset this month, even though it is a
reversion to historical and international practice. The card schemes used to impose “no-surcharge”
rules, until the RBA banned these in 2003, and many other countries still have them. The turnaround
represents the RBA throwing in the towel on its longstanding effort to use price incentives to get people
to choose low-cost payment methods. Surcharges no longer made sense in a largely cashless
economy. In any case, least-cost routing puts the power to choose lower-cost methods in the hands of
the merchants who actually bear the cost.

In most cases, firms will simply roll the surcharge into the sticker price, leaving the total cost to the
consumer unchanged, just more transparent. We also understand that some large retailers have
absorbed the cost – if they were surcharging in the first place, which many did not. (The RBA estimated
that around 16% of businesses imposed surcharges.) Inevitably, though, some firms have lifted their
prices by more than any reasonable estimate of the merchant service fee that the surcharge was
meant to cover. And because this is Australia, of course takeaway coffee has been the centre of
consumer discontent.

Higher prices for one’s caffeine fix was unlikely to be the main instigator of this month’s slump in
consumer sentiment. Petrol prices are typically more salient for cost-of-living concerns, and the before-
and-after picture on the RBA announcement that week shows that higher interest rates also contributed
to the decline.

In some senses, though, the post-surcharge prices and the RBA’s decisions are linked: businesses
passing on increased costs into their own prices – and expecting to be able to make that increase stick
– is exactly the kind of inflationary environment the RBA is leaning against. Firms have been subjected
to an array of non-labour cost escalations, from pandemic-era supply chain issues to electricity to fuel
to water to building supplies. And IT equipment prices, which for decades were a reliable source of cost
reductions, are nowadays flat to up. Passing these costs on, leaving margins intact, demonstrates that
demand is not weak enough to prevent this, and signals that firms’ inflationary expectations might be
higher than the RBA desires. The RBA will take this as a sign that demand is still outstripping supply.

To the extent they can pass on these costs through to prices, it suggests that demand was strong
enough to bear it. The RBA will take this as a signal that demand is still outstripping supply.
This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. Base wages growth has been moderate for some
years, though the above-average increase in award wages this year will add a little. Not all firms will
seek to pass these cost increases on, but some will, including because they have limited scope to
absorb the cost increase into margins.
A cost-plus mentality has drawbacks, including that it limits the imagination away from finding
alternative, efficiency-improving responses. It also accords with recent academic research
suggesting that firms form expectations and set prices according to what is happening in their own
industry and experience, while putting little weight on economy-wide inflation or central bank
credibility. This is an unpleasant message for many consumers or anyone in an interest-sensitive
sector when supply shocks abound.

### Page 3

This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. While the Fair Work Commission did hand down an
above-average increase in award wages this year and this was probably passed through to prices in
hospitality and other labour-intensive sectors, base wages growth has been moderate for some years.
Business surveys point to non-labour cost escalation being the real issue lately. This is why we were
puzzled by the passage in the RBA minutes this week suggesting that cost pressures reported in
business surveys might imply that the unemployment rate consistent with inflation at target (the NAIRU)
has risen. It is a convenient belief, though, if unemployment is starting to overshoot your forecasts, as
the latest labour market data have done compared with the RBA’s August forecasts.

Not all firms can pass on cost increases. Most small firms do not have the market power, though coffee
may be an exception because convenience induces a kind of local market power. And as noted above,
large firms with everyday-low-price strategies will instead try to find savings elsewhere in the supply
chain.

Still, some firms will seek to pass these cost increases on. Some observers might see this as corporate
greed, but another way to frame this strategy is simply as a ‘cost-plus’ mentality. It turns out that cost-
plus pricing is especially common in areas where regulators determine the price – water and other
utilities being examples where costs and prices have escalated a lot in recent years.

There are limits to firms’ ability to absorb costs, too. The failures of so many building firms a few years
ago, squeezed between post-pandemic cost escalation, delivery delays and fixed-price contracts, were
a case in point. Normally, there is a contractual response, and it need not end in insolvency. The
episode does illustrate, however, that it is unrealistic to expect firms never to pass cost increases
through to prices.

The cost-plus mentality does have its drawbacks. It limits the imagination away from finding responses
that might save costs elsewhere to absorb the cost hike, and in so doing, boost efficiency. It was
equally limiting in times of soft demand: in the years before the pandemic, many firms professed a
belief that, because they couldn’t get price increases to stick, they could not possibly accept cost
increases, including in wages. Thus, Australia ended up with wages growth too low to be consistent
with inflation at target, and the peculiar spectacle of a central bank governor complaining that wages
growth was too low. And if labour remains cheap, why invest to economise on it and thereby boost
productivity?

A cost-plus mentality also amplifies the pass-through of supply shocks. This is not quite the same thing
as the ‘inflation expectations’ seen in standard economic models of inflation, but it is related. The
stronger the tendency towards cost-plus pricing, the worse the inflation response to a supply shock.
This is also true when inflation expectations are high, but the distinction matters. The recent academic
literature includes some fascinating empirical evidence from New Zealand that suggests that beliefs
about current and future overall inflation have little bearing on many firms’ pricing decisions. As related
work using French data shows, firms’ pricing choices are shaped less by economy-wide inflation
developments than by what is happening to their costs and in their industry.

### Page 4

Related articles
That adds up to economy-wide behaviour that looks a lot like firms (in aggregate) setting prices
according to expected inflation (in aggregate), but under the hood something quite different is going on.
If firms’ beliefs about future economy-wide inflation do not matter much in their pricing decisions, then
neither does the credibility of a central bank’s commitment to return inflation to target. The mechanism
to get inflation down then must be simply to weaken demand enough to convince firms that cost-plus
will not fly in the market. This is an unpleasant message for many consumers or anyone in an interest-
sensitive sector when supply shocks abound. It does, however, imply that if supply shocks fade or even
self-correct, so will high inflation. If and when that happens, tight monetary policy can be unwound.

The deeper question is whether cost-plus price dynamics can be reduced. That is more likely to be a
question for regulators than for central banks.

## Document 411: 「社外取締役が議長」は何を変えるのか ～プライム市場で118社、大型企業で選任進む～

Source PDF: all report pdf/「社外取締役が議長」は何を変えるのか ～プライム市場で118社、大型企業で選任進む～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Flash Insights

2026 年 10 月 8 日

1/4 
「社外取締役が議長」は何を変えるのか 
～プライム市場で 118 社、大型企業で選任進む～ 
 資産運用調査部 シニア研究員  河谷 善夫

1．社外取締役の取締役会議長選任は緩やかに拡大 
筆者は、昨年8 月中旬時点でのプライム市場上場会社のうち、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選
任している会社について分析したレポート（河谷、2025）を公表し、2022 年 7 月上旬時点より、
その数が増加していることを示した。本稿では、2026 年9 月末の時点でのプライム市場における
社外取締役の取締役会議長選任の状況をフォローアップした上で、最後に社外取締役の取締役会
議長選任の意味について考える。

資料 1 社外取締役の取締役会議長選任会社数の推移

（出所）東証公表資料を基に第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

資料 1 のとおり、2025 年 8 月から2026 年 9 月にかけて会社数は9 社増加し、プライム市場での
比率は 6.7％から7.6％と0.9％ポイント上昇した。2022 年以降、年平均で概ね1％ポイントの上
昇ペースが続いている。このグラフだけでは見えないが、2025 年8 月から2026 年9 月にかけて継
続してプライム市場に上場している会社では17 社が社外取締役を新たに取締役会議長に選任して
おり（注1）、新規選任の動きは続いている。 
また、118 名中 117 名が独立社外取締役であり、社外取締役議長はほぼすべて独立社外取締役で
ある（注2）。

### Page 2

2/4 
2．特定の機関設計に限らず普及、ただし社外取締役議長はなお少数 
資料 2 は、会社の機関設計別の2022 年、2025 年、2026 年のプライム市場での社外取締役の取
締役会議長選任会社数と割合を示したものである。

資料 2 機関設計別の社外取締役の取締役会議長選任状況（プライム市場）

（出所）資料 1 と同じ

2026 年では、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任している会社は、監査役設置会社で5.3％、監
査等委員会設置会社で6.0％、指名委員会等設置会社で42.7％である。指名委員会等設置会社の
割合は依然として高いが、2022 年からの増加状況をみると、社外取締役を取締役会議長とする動
きは、指名委員会等設置会社以外にも徐々に広まっており、昨年のレポートで確認した傾向は続
いているといえる。 
なお、これも資料で示していないが、取締役の過半を独立社外取締役とする会社でも、社外取
締役を取締役会議長とする会社は約2 割にとどまり、2025 年 8 月から2026 年 9 月にかけて大きな
変化はなかった（注3）。

3．社長を議長とする会社の減少と、大型企業で先行する社外取締役議長選任 
資料 3 では、2022 年、2025 年、2026 年のプライム市場での取締役会議長の属性の変化をグラフ
として示した。

資料 3 取締役会議長の属性の変化（プライム市場）

（注）「その他の取締役等」には、会長・社長以外の執行役を兼任する取締役を含む。 
（出所）資料 1 と同じ

### Page 3

3/4 
2022 年からの変化をみると、社長を取締役会議長とする会社の割合は71.4％から65.6％へ
5.8％ポイント低下する一方、社外取締役を議長とする会社の割合は、3.6％から 7.6％へ4.0％ポ
イント上昇し、会長を議長とする会社の割合も1.5％ポイント上昇している。このような社長を取
締役会議長とする会社の割合の低下、社外取締役を議長とする会社の割合の上昇は、取締役会議
長と社長を分離するという、より大きな流れの一部とみることができるだろう。 
次に、どのような会社で社外取締役の取締役会議長選任が進んでいるのかをみるため、2026 年
9 月末時点のプライム市場上場会社について、時価総額ランク別の選任割合を示した（資料4）。

資料 4 時価総額ランク別社外取締役議長選任割合（プライム市場・2026 年 9 月末）

（出所）Bloomberg、東証公表資料データを基に第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

資料 4 からは、時価総額の大きい企業ほど、社外取締役の取締役会議長選任が進んでいるとい
える。なお、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任している会社の社数の割合は7.6％だが、時価総額
では 19％を占める。また、社外取締役が議長となっている会社の39％が時価総額１兆円以上の会
社である。時価総額が大きい企業ほど選任率が高く、大型企業での社外取締役の取締役会議長選
任が相対的に進んでいる。

4．最後に 
ここまで、社外取締役の取締役会議長選任状況について確認してきた。このような社外取締役
の取締役会議長選任はコーポレートガバナンス上、どのような意味を持つのか、最後に私見を述
べたい。 
とりわけ独立社外取締役が議長を務める場合には、独立した立場から取締役会の議論を促し、
監督機能を含む取締役会の実効性向上につながることが期待される。そのためには、経営陣との
適切な連絡・調整や十分な情報提供が重要となる。今回のコーポレートガバナンス・コード改訂
でも、議長や独立社外取締役を支える取締役会事務局（コーポレートセクレタリー等）の機能強

### Page 4

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
4/4 
化の重要性が示されている。社外取締役を議長に選任する企業には、選任によってどのような効
果を目指すのかを明確にし、それに即した支援体制を整えることが重要であろう。 
以 上

1) 2025 年8 月中旬のプライム市場上場会社（1,618 社）のうち、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選
任している会社数の異同については次のとおり。2026 年 9 月末までの間にプライム市場から退

社）で社外取締役以外を取締役会議長にした会社1 社の計8 社が減少。継続上場会社のうち17 社
が新規に社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任したので、差し引き9 社の増加となった。新規にプライ

## Document 459: インド中銀、ルピー防衛に向けて異例の措置を相次ぎ導入 ～原油高と資金流出による通貨安圧力は残る、外貨準備の減少や企業負担の増加に懸念～

Source PDF: all report pdf/インド中銀、ルピー防衛に向けて異例の措置を相次ぎ導入 ～原油高と資金流出による通貨安圧力は残る、外貨準備の減少や企業負担の増加に懸念～.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 5

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 15 日

1/5 
インド中銀、ルピー防衛に向けて異例の措置を相次ぎ導入 
～原油高と資金流出による通貨安圧力は残る、外貨準備の減少や企業負担の増加に懸念～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ イラン情勢の悪化に伴う原油高や、米国の金利上昇を背景とする資金流出により、インドル
ピーの下落圧力が強まっている。こうしたなか、インド準備銀行（ＲＢＩ）は 10 月 10～12
日にかけて、国営石油会社への特別ドル供給制度の創設や為替デリバティブ取引の規制強化
など、一連のルピー防衛策を打ち出した。これらの措置は、石油会社によるドル買い需要や
投機的なルピー売りを抑制する狙いがあり、発表後のルピー相場は反発する動きをみせてい
る。一方、外貨準備の減少や企業の為替ヘッジ費用の増加、金融市場の流動性低下などの副
作用も懸念される。６月に導入されたルピー支援策も過剰流動性を招いた経緯があり、今回
の措置による影響にも注意が必要と考えられる。さらに、原油高や米国の金利高止まりとい
ったルピー安の根本的な要因は解消されておらず、 対策の効果が持続するかは不透明である。
ＲＢＩにとっては、先行きもインフレ抑制、景気の下支え、通貨価値の安定という複数の政
策目標の間で、一段と難しい政策運営を迫られる展開が予想される。

【原油高や資金流出を背景にルピー安圧力が強まり、ＲＢＩは対応を迫られる展開に直面】 
インド準備銀行（ＲＢＩ）は10 月 10 日、通貨ルピーの下落圧力を抑制すべく、国営石油会社向
けのドル供給制度の創設や、 為替デリバティブ取引に対する規制強化など、 一連の措置を発表した。
さらに、10 月 12 日にも追加規制を発表しており、ルピー相場の安定に向けて対応を強化する姿勢
を鮮明にした。背景には、イラン情勢の悪化を受けた原油価格の高止まりや、米国の金利上昇を受
けたドル高を背景とする資金流出がある。 インドは原油需要の大部分を輸入に依存しており、 原油
高は貿易赤字の拡大を通じてルピー安圧力を強める要因となる。 こうしたなか、ＲＢＩが一連の措
置を発表する直前のルピー相場は、過去最安値に迫る水準で推移していた。

### Page 2

2/5 
ＲＢＩは、これまでも外国為替市場で
のドル売り介入などを通じてルピー相場
の安定を図ってきた。10 月７日の定例金
融政策委員会では、政策金利であるレポ
金利を 25bp 引き上げて 5.50％とすると
ともに（図１）、 政策スタンスも中立から
「 調 整 さ れ た 引 き 締 め （calibrated 
tightening） 」 に変更した（注１）。ＲＢＩ
は追加利上げに含みを持たせる一方、そ
の時期やペースを明確に示さなかったた
め、 金融市場においてはＲＢＩによる 「次
の一手」が読みにくいとの観測が残った。加えて、原油高や海外投資家による資金流出が続いたこ
とも重なり、ルピー安圧力を十分に抑制するには至らなかった。このため、ＲＢＩは従来から実施
してきた為替介入や金融引き締めに加え、国内におけるドルの需給構造に直接働きかける対応を打
ち出したと捉えられる。

【国営石油会社向けドル供給と為替デリバティブ規制を組み合わせた対策】 
今回の措置の柱のひとつが、国営石油会社に対する特別ドル供給制度の創設である。ＲＢＩは、
インディアン・オイル、ヒンドスタン・ペトロリアム、バーラト・ペトロリアムの国営石油会社３
社について、10 月 12 日から日々のドル需要を全額満たす仕組みを導入した。具体的には、ＲＢＩ
が指定銀行を通じて３社に直接ドルを売却することにより、 外国為替市場におけるドル買い需要を
抑制する。原油価格の高騰に伴い石油会
社によるドル需要が膨らんでおり、金融
市場から主要なドル需要主体を切り離す
ことにより、ルピー安圧力を緩和する狙
いがある。 ただし、今回の措置を通じて、
輸入代金の支払いに必要なドル需要その
ものが減少するわけではない。ＲＢＩが
保有する外貨準備からドルが供給される
ため、外貨準備の減少を通じて先行きの
政策対応の余地が狭まる可能性には注意
が必要である（図２）。 
さらに、ＲＢＩは為替デリバティブ取引の規制も強化した。具体的には、一度解約したルピー関

3/5 
連の為替デリバティブ契約について、 再予約を原則禁止したほか、 実際の取引に伴う為替リスクの
存在を証明せずに取引できる上限額を、 従来の１億ドルから500 万ドルに大幅に引き下げた。銀行
には、 企業が同一の実需を裏付けとして複数の金融機関で重複したヘッジ取引を行っていないこと
を確認するよう義務付けた。加えて、200 万ドルを上回る額の一定の為替デリバティブ取引につい
ては、銀行に対して取引額の 20％に相当する現金をＲＢＩに預け入れる「外国為替リスク準備金
（FERR） 」 制度を導入した。 こうした措置には、 実需を上回る投機的なドル買いや、 ルピー安を見込
んだ過度なヘッジ取引を抑制する狙いがある。 
10 月 12 日には、規制の対象がさらに拡大された。ＲＢＩは、非居住者向けのルピー関連ノンデ
リバラブル・デリバティブ契約について、ヘッジ目的に限定することを決定した。加えて、銀行自
身の資産・負債に伴う為替リスクをヘッジする一定の取引にも、20％の FERR を課すこととした。
国内の現物市場にとどまらず、 デリバティブ市場や海外投資家の取引にも規制を広げることで、一
方向のルピー売り圧力を抑え込もうとする姿勢がうかがえる。

【ルピー相場は一時的に反発も、 外貨準備や企業の為替ヘッジに副作用が生じる可能性には要注意】 
ＲＢＩによる一連の措置の発表 を受け
て、その後のルピー相場は反発する動き
をみせている（図３）。 国営石油会社によ
るドル需要が市場から取り除かれる 実
需面の動きに加え、先行きもルピー安が
続くと 見込 む投機的な取引が抑制され
るとの見方が、相場を押し上げていると
考えられる。ルピー相場の安定は、原油
などの輸入価格の上昇圧力を緩和する
ことを通じて、インフレ抑制にも寄与す
ると期待される。 
一方で、今回の措置には副作用も懸念される。特に FERR の導入は、銀行が為替ヘッジ取引に応
じる際の資金負担を増加させるため、 銀行がその費用を企業に転嫁する可能性がある。 輸入企業に
とっては、 ルピー安による輸入コスト上昇を回避するためのヘッジ費用が高まり、 経営負担が増す
おそれがある。 さらに、 ヘッジ費用の増加によって企業が為替リスクを十分に回避できなくなれば、
将来的にルピー安が再燃した際の損失が拡大する可能性もある。 また、 銀行の資金がＲＢＩに預け
入れられることに伴い、 金融市場の流動性は吸収されるため、 金融機関の資金調達や企業向け融資
にも間接的な影響が及ぶことが考えられる。

4/5 
なお、ＲＢＩは景気に悪影響を与える利上げを回避するため、６月会合でルピー相場の安定を目
的とする支援策を発表した。具体的には、非居住インド人に対する優遇ドル預金制度、企業・金融
機関に対する対外商業借入のヘッジコスト補助を導入した。 政府も、 適格外国人投資家による国債
売却益に対するキャピタルゲイン税撤廃
を発表するなど、海外からの資金流入を
促すことでルピー相場を下支えする方針
を明らかにした。その後は海外からの資
金流入が活発化し、ルピー相場は一時的
に持ち直した。しかし、資金流入の活発
化により国内銀行ではドル建て預金が急
拡大し、各行は中銀とのスワップを通じ
てコストなしでルピーに交換したため、
銀行システムでは過剰流動性を招く「副
作用」 を招いた （注２） 。 ＲＢＩは過剰流
動性がインフレ圧力を増幅させることを警戒して、 資金吸収オペ （売りオペ） を活発化させたほか、
為替スワップを通じてルピー資金の吸収を図った。 その結果、 ＲＢＩによる金融引き締めの動きも
重なり、主要株価指数 （ムンバイSENSEX） の上値が抑えられてきた。 今回の一連の措置でも副作用
の影響には注意が必要である。

【短期的には効果が期待されるも、根本的な問題が残るなかで政策対応は困難が増す展開が続く 】 
加えて、 今回の措置がルピー安の根本的な要因を解消するわけではない点にも注意が必要である。
イラン情勢の悪化に伴う原油高が長期化すれば、 インドの輸入負担は一段と増加し、 経常収支の悪
化がルピー相場の重石となる ことが懸念
される。 さらに、 米国の市場金利が高止ま
りするなかでは、新興国からの資金流出
圧力も続きやすい。 
このところの原油価格の再上昇を受け
て、９月の卸売物価上昇率は前年同月比
＋11.4％と高止まりしており、消費者物
価上昇率も同＋5.54％と 2024 年 10 月以
来の高い伸びとなっている（図５）。この
ため、先行きはＲＢＩによる追加利上げ
実施が意識され 、ルピー安によるインフ

## Document 472: スペイン、前倒しの総選挙へ ～政権交代なら住宅・移民政策を軌道修正～

Source PDF: all report pdf/スペイン、前倒しの総選挙へ ～政権交代なら住宅・移民政策を軌道修正～.pdf

スペイン、前倒しの総選挙へ 
～政権交代なら住宅・移民政策を軌道修正～ 
 経済調査部 首席エコノミスト  田中 理 
（℡：050-5474-7494）

（要旨） 
○ スペインのサンチェス首相は10 月５日、 議会を解散し、11 月 29 日に前倒し総選挙を実施す
ると発表した。少数政権による議会運営は予算や住宅政策を巡って行き詰まり、カタルーニ
ャ独立派政党との関係悪化も政権運営を難しくしていた。足元の世論調査では中道右派の国
民党（ＰＰ）が第１党、右派ポピュリストのボックス（Vox）が第３党につけ、ＰＰ単独では
なくVox の協力を前提とした政権発足の可能性が高い。 政権交代となれば、 住宅政策は規制 ・
借家人保護重視から供給拡大や規制緩和へ軸足を移し、 移民政策も厳格化する可能性がある。
特に Vox の影響力が強まれば、人口・労働力の増加に支えられてきたスペインの成長モデル
や、ＥＵ・カタルーニャとの関係にも変化が及ぶ可能性がある。

■少数政権の行き詰まり、住宅問題が解散の引き金に 
スペインのサンチェス首相は10 月５日、議会を解散し、11 月 29 日に前倒し総選挙を実施する
と発表した。2027 年までの議会任期を全うするとしてきた従来の方針を転換したもので、2023 年
の総選挙後に続いてきた少数政権による不安定な議会運営はひとまず終止符を打つことになる。
今回の解散・総選挙は、少数与党による議会運営の行き詰まりに、与党関係者を巡る様々な疑
惑、カタルーニャ独立派の地域政党との関係悪化、住宅危機への国民の不満などが重なり、政府
が重要政策を成立させることが難しくなったことが背景にある。 
2023 年の総選挙後に発足したサンチェス政権は、中道左派の「社会労働党（PSOE）」と左派の
「スマール（Sumar）」による連立政権だが、両党だけでは下院の過半数を確保できない。そのた
め、政権発足時には、「カタルーニャ共和主義左翼（ＥＲＣ）」、「ともにカタルーニャのために
（Junts）」、「バスク民族党（ＰＮＶ）」、「バスク統一党（Bildu）」など、カタルーニャやバスクを
中心とする地域政党から協力を取り付けた。なかでも、サンチェス政権の存続に重要な役割を果
たしてきたのがJuntsである。2023 年の政権発足時には、2017 年のカタルーニャの独立運動を巡
って有罪判決を受けた政治家や活動家を対象とする恩赦を条件に、サンチェス首相の続投に協力
したが、連立政権には参加せず、その後も個々の法案毎に賛否を判断してきた。このため、少数
の議席を持つ地域政党が政府の法案成立を左右する不安定な状況が続いてきた。 
政権運営の機能不全を象徴しているのが予算だ。政府は2023 年予算を最後に新たな予算を成立

### Page 2

2/3 
させることができず、過去３年は既存予算の延長を余儀なくされてきた。2026 年７月には、2027
年予算編成の前提となる財政安定化計画も、中道右派の「国民党（ＰＰ） 」、右派ポピュリストの
「ボックス（Vox）」、Juntsなどの反対で議会で否決された。閣僚経験者、党幹部、首相の家族な
ど、与党関係者を巡る疑惑が相次いで表面化したことも、政権への風当たりを強めた。それで
も、サンチェス首相は2027 年までの議会任期を全うする方針を維持してきた。 
こうした状況を大きく変えたのが住宅問題だ。スペインでは人口や世帯数の増加に住宅供給が
追いつかず、住宅価格と家賃の上昇が大きな社会問題となっている。９月下旬にマドリードで87
歳の女性が長年暮らしてきた住居から立ち退かされたことを契機に抗議活動が広がり、住宅問題
が一気に政治争点として浮上した（詳細は10 月１日付けレポート「スペイン高成長の裏で深まる
住宅危機」を参照されたい）。政府は立ち退き停止措置の延長や賃貸市場の規制強化などを盛り込
んだ緊急政令を議会に提出したが、ＰＰやVoxなどの野党勢力に加えて、Juntsも反対に回った結
果、承認されなかった。Juntsは借家人保護や賃貸規制の強化が住宅所有者を賃貸市場から退出さ
せ、結果的に供給不足や家賃上昇を深刻化させると主張した。サンチェス首相が総選挙の前倒し
を決断した背景には、こうした議会運営の行き詰まりがある。同時に、住宅危機への国民の関心
が急速に高まるなか、住宅政策を選挙の中心的な争点に据えることで、劣勢の挽回を図る狙いも
あるとみられる。

■政権交代の可能性が高い、Voxの政策関与に注目 
 各種の世論調査では、ＰＰが30％台前半の支持を集めてリードし、それを20％台後半のPSOEが
追い、Voxが20％近くで三番手につけている（図）。PSOEが現政権で連立を組むSumarは５～６％前
後で低迷している。前倒し総選挙の正式発表後に実施された世論調査はまだ公表されていない。
このまま世論調査に近い結果となれば、PSOEがＰＰに政権を明け渡すことになりそうだ。だが、
ＰＰも単独で過半数を確保するのは難しく、安定政権の樹立にはVoxの協力が必要となる公算が大
きい。Voxが連立政権の一員に加わるのか、それとも閣外からＰＰ政権を支えるのかによって、政
策決定への関与の度合いが変わってくる。既に複数の州政府で、ＰＰが主導する政権にVoxが連立
パートナーとして参加している。連立政権に加わる場合、ＰＰのフェイホー党首が首相に就任
し、Voxが副首相や閣僚ポストを獲得する形が考えられる。閣外協力にとどまる場合、政権発足時
や重要法案の審議でＰＰ政権を支えることになる。 
ＰＰが主導する政権が誕生した場合、PSOE政権の公的介入路線が軌道修正され、減税や規制緩
和を通じた経済活性化を目指す可能性が高い。ＰＰのフェイホー党首は、年間20 万戸規模の住宅
建設、土地供給の拡大、建設手続きの簡素化、住宅関連税制の軽減などを掲げている。移民政策
の転換も予想される。サンチェス政権は移民の受け入れに比較的寛容で、移民による人口と労働
力の増加は近年のスペインの高成長を支える重要な要因となってきた。移民規制が強まれば、住
宅需要への圧力を幾分弱める可能性がある反面、労働供給の増加ペースが鈍化する。観光、建
設、農業、介護など移民労働力への依存度が高い業種では人手不足や賃金上昇圧力が強まること
が予想される。特にVoxは、不法移民の強制送還、移民への給付制限、長期滞在資格の厳格化な

### Page 3

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
3/3 
ど、移民政策の強化を主張している。ＰＰとVoxが連立を組む州政府では、住宅や公共サービスで
スペイン国民を優先する考えが盛り込まれた例もある。 
 ＰＰが主導する政権の誕生は、カタルーニャ独立派との対話路線が転換され、独立の火種を抱
えるカタルーニャ州政府との関係を再び悪化させる恐れがある。ＰＰがかつて率いた政権では、
領土的一体性や法の執行を重視し、カタルーニャの独立問題に厳しく対処した。次期政権とカタ
ルーニャ州政府との緊張が再び高まれば、金融市場でスペインの政治リスクが意識される可能性
がある。 
 Voxの政権参加によるＥＵ関係の悪化を懸念する声もあるが、ＰＰ自体は長年スペインの政権を
率いてきた親ＥＵの中道右派政党で、政権交代によってスペインのＥＵ政策が大幅に変わること
は想定しにくい。ただ、Voxの政策面での影響力が増すことで、移民政策、気候変動政策、農業政
策などでは、スペイン政府とＥＵ機関との摩擦が増える可能性がある。 
現時点でスペインの政治リスクをフランスと同列に扱う必要はない。スペイン経済は相対的に
高い成長を維持しており、今回の解散も財政危機や景気後退を背景としたものではない。むし
ろ、市場にとって重要なのは、総選挙によってこれまでの議会の機能不全が解消され、安定した
政権が成立するかどうかである。次期政権が安定多数を確保すれば、予算を成立させられなかっ
た現政権に比べ、短期的には政策の予見可能性が改善する。一方で、Voxの影響力が大きくなるほ
ど、移民政策、気候変動対策、カタルーニャ政策、ＥＵ政策での変化が大きくなる。特に移民政
策の厳格化は、人口増加に支えられてきたスペインの成長モデルに中期的な変化をもたらす。

　た（SALF）、カタルーニャ共和主義左翼（ERC）、ともにカタルーニャ
　のために（Junts）、バスク統一党（Bildu）、バスク民族党（PNV）、
